[Director 指令] 紧急任务：R14 审稿要求的两个关键实验。这是从 3.0→3.5 的唯一升级路径。

**优先级排序（覆盖之前的 pending 实验队列）：**
1. **C2: OPT-1.3B at α=0.50**（最高优先级）
2. **C1: N=5 Intervention**（第二优先级）
3. 之前 pending 的 14b/t15/qwen 实验降为低优先级

---

## C2: OPT-1.3B at α=0.50, T=10, 2 seeds

**审稿人原话**："如果跨架构验证扩展到 α∈[0.50, 0.75] 的过渡区域，评分可升至 3.5"

**为什么选 OPT-1.3B**：
- GPT-2-medium standalone null FPR = 50%（6/12），不适合做验证
- OPT-1.3B null FPR 干净，已有 α=1.0 数据 (6/8 forward detection)
- 如果 OPT 在 α=0.50 也检测到 onset，直接验证 phase diagram 结构在非 Pythia 上的泛化性

**实验配置**：
- Model: OPT-1.3B (facebook/opt-1.3b)
- Dataset: WikiText-103
- α = 0.50 (synthetic fraction)
- T = 10 (generations)
- Seeds: 2 个（s42, s43）
- Precision: fp32
- 其他超参与 Pythia-410M 主实验一致

**预估 GPU 时间**：~8h/seed × 2 seeds = ~16h

**执行步骤**：
1. 先检查 null_fpr_s51_fp32 是否已完成或接近完成。如果还在跑，先准备 C2 代码
2. 在服务器 westd-19828 上准备 OPT-1.3B 实验代码（参考已有的 cross-arch 实验脚本）
3. s51 完成后立即提交 C2 实验（submit_training_job）
4. C2 两个 seed 可以串行跑（只有 1 GPU）

**产出**：
- 每个 seed 的 PermG 检测结果（forward/reverse pairs）
- Metric trajectories（entropy, distinct-1/2/3, PPL）
- 与 Pythia-410M α=0.50 结果对比

---

## C1: N=5 Intervention（C2 之后执行）

之前已有计划和代码准备。3 个架构：
1. GPT-2-medium (~10h)
2. OPT-1.3B (~20h) — 可以与 C2 共用 OPT α=1.0 训练管线
3. Qwen2.5-0.8B (~12h)

C2 完成后依次执行。

---

先汇报 null_fpr_s51 的当前状态。

  - Generated Script: `/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0514_4/projects/distrib_canary_collapse/configs/sprint_day1/plan_030_opt_s43.sh`

**Key Command Arguments for OPT-1.3B**:
```bash
--model_name facebook/opt-1.3b
--seed 43
--data_dir data/wikitext103_5k
--steps_per_gen 5000
--num_generations 10
--synthetic_ratio 1.0
--gradient_checkpointing
--output_dir results/generalize/opt1b_wiki_a100_s43
```

### 2. **Main Training Script Location**

**Primary locations** (multiple copies exist):
- `/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0514_4/projects/distrib_canary_collapse/artifacts/iterative_train.py` (11.5 KB, main version with `--accumulate` flag support)
- `/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0514_4/projects/distrib_canary_collapse/artifacts/_project/intermediate/iterative_train.py` (10.2 KB, intermediate version)

**Core Command-Line Arguments**:
- `--model_name`: Model identifier (e.g., `facebook/opt-1.3b`, `EleutherAI/pythia-410m`, `EleutherAI/pythia-1.4b`)
- `--data_dir`: Path to prepared datasets (train/eval/prompts splits)
- `--output_dir`: Results directory
- `--num_generations`: Number of iterative training generations (default 10)
- `--steps_per_gen`: Training steps per generation (default 5000)
- `--synthetic_ratio`: Fraction of synthetic data to mix (0.0-1.0, default 1.0)
- `--seed`: Random seed for reproducibility
- `--batch_size`: Per-device batch size (default 4)
- `--lr`: Learning rate (default 5e-5)
- `--max_length`: Sequence length (default 512)
- `--gen_max_new_tokens`: Max tokens for generation sampling (default 256)
- `--gen_batch_size`: Batch size for synthetic data generation (default 16)
- `--top_p`: Nucleus sampling parameter (default 0.95)
- `--gradient_checkpointing`: Enable for large models (1.3B+)
- `--accumulate`: Pool all historical synthetic data instead of only latest generation

### 3. **Cross-Architecture Experiment Configs**

Found in `/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0514_4/projects/distrib_canary_collapse/configs/sprint_day1/_generate.py`:

**Architectures tested**:
- **GPT2-medium**: `openai-community/gpt2-medium` (plan_034)
- **OPT-1.3B**: `facebook/opt-1.3b` (plan_030) 
- **Pythia-410m**: `EleutherAI/pythia-410m` (core baseline, multiple alpha sweep plans)
- **Pythia-1.4B**: `EleutherAI/pythia-1.4b` (plan_036, plan_037a/b with intervention modes)

**Datasets tested**:
- `wikitext103_5k` (primary)
- `c4_5k` (secondary, cross-domain validation)

**Cross-architecture visualization**:
- `/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0514_4/projects/distrib_canary_collapse/docs/paper/figures/gen_fig_cross_arch_heatmap.py` — Reads `artifacts/cross_arch_table7.csv` and plots PermG forward detection rates across model families

### 4. **Analysis/Detection Pipeline (PermG)**

**Permutation Granger Causality Implementation**:

Core module:
- `/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0514_4/projects/distrib_canary_collapse/artifacts/permutation_granger.py` (standalone implementation)

Key function signature:
```python
def permutation_granger_test(x, y, max_lag=1, n_perm=1000, seed=42, difference=True)
```

Returns:
- `f_stat`: Granger F-statistic
- `p_value`: Empirical p-value from permutation test
- `significant`: Boolean (p < 0.05)
- `effect_size_f2`: Coefficient of determination

**Power Analysis**:
- `/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0514_4/projects/distrib_canary_collapse/scripts/permg_power_analysis.py` — Monte Carlo simulation for power curves across signal strength (beta) and series length (T)

**Post-Training Analysis Scripts** (`run_analysis.py` templates):
Each experiment directory contains a `run_analysis.py` implementing the **5-method lead-lag analysis**:

Example locations:
- `/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0514_4/projects/distrib_canary_collapse/artifacts/d102_14b_t10_a075_s42_fp32/run_analysis.py`
- `/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0514_4/projects/distrib_canary_collapse/artifacts/alpha_sweep_a060_s42_fp32_v4/run_analysis.py`
- `/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0514_4/projects/distrib_canary_collapse/artifacts/phase_diagram_t20_a075_s43_fp32/run_analysis.py`

**5 Detection Methods**:
1. **Permutation Granger (PermG)**: Primary test with BH FDR correction across 8 metric pairs
2. **Naive Cross-Correlation**: Peak Pearson r across lags (-5 to +5)
3. **Differenced Cross-Correlation**: On first-differenced series
4. **Threshold Onset**: 3-sigma detection of collapse onset
5. **VAR Granger**: Vector autoregression-based causality

**Metrics System**:
- `/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0514_4/projects/distrib_canary_collapse/artifacts/metrics.py` — Compute all per-generation metrics

Canary metrics (collapse indicators):
- `token_entropy`: Next-token entropy on held-out data
- `ece`: Expected Calibration Error

Downstream metrics (effects of collapse):
- `distinct_1`, `distinct_2`, `distinct_3`: N-gram diversity
- `mauve`: Machine learning evaluation metric (text quality)
- `perplexity`, `effective_rank`, `self_bleu`

**Usage pattern**: Each `run_analysis.py` reads metrics from `gen_0/metrics.json` through `gen_N/metrics.json`, performs lead-lag analysis, and outputs results (p-values, effect sizes, detected causality direction).

### File Structure Summary

搜索 artifacts/ 下包含 \"a000\" 或 \"null\" 的 run_analysis.py\n   - 例如: /home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0514_4/projects/distrib_canary_collapse/artifacts/ 下面的 null 相关目录\n   - 关键: 分析脚本需要计算 5 种方法 (NaiveXCorr, DiffXCorr, ThresholdOnset, PermG, Toda-Yamamoto) 的 forward/reverse detection\n   - 每种方法检测 8 个 canary→downstream pairs\n\n3. **在服务器上运行分析** (ssh_execute)\n   - 复制已有的 run_analysis.py 到 s51 结果目录\n   - 修改输入路径指向 a000_s51_fp32\n   - 运行分析脚本 (CPU only)\n   - 收集结果: 每种方法的 FDR-significant forward/reverse pair 数量\n\n4. **收集分析结果**\n   读取分析输出，记录:\n   - PermG forward FDR-significant pairs: ?/8\n   - PermG reverse FDR-significant pairs: ?/8\n   - 其他4种方法的 forward/reverse counts\n   - 所有原始 p-values\n\n5. **合并 FPR 统计**\n   当前 9-seed null FPR (from paper):\n   - PermG: 4/54 = 7.4%, 95% CI [2.1%, 17.9%]\n   - 7/9 seeds yield 0 FP\n   \n   加入 s51 后变成 10 seeds (60 pairs total):\n   - 新 FPR = (4 + s51_FP) / 60\n   - 用 Clopper-Pearson exact binomial CI 计算新 95% CI:\n   ```python\n   from scipy.stats import binom\n   n = 60\n   k = 4 + s51_fp  # s51 的 FP 数\n   ci_low = binom.ppf(0.025, n, k/n) / n if k > 0 else 0\n   ci_high = binom.ppf(0.975, n, k/n) / n\n   # 或用 statsmodels:\n   from statsmodels.stats.proportion import proportion_confint\n   ci = proportion_confint(k, n, method='beta')\n   ```\n\n6. **产出** — 在本地创建 artifact:\n   写入 /home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0514_4/projects/distrib_canary_collapse/artifacts/null_fpr_s51_analysis.json:\n   ```json\n   {\n     \"seed\": 51,\n     \"permg_fwd_fdr\": X,\n     \"permg_rev_fdr\": X,\n     \"other_methods\": {...},\n     \"fpr_10seed\": {\n       \"permg_fp_total\": X,\n       \"permg_total_pairs\": 60,\n       \"permg_fpr\": X,\n       \"permg_ci_95\": [low, high],\n       \"seeds_with_zero_fp\": \"?/10\"\n     },\n     \"fpr_9seed_previous\": {\n       \"permg_fp_total\": 4,\n       \"permg_total_pairs\": 54,\n       \"permg_fpr\": 0.074,\n       \"permg_ci_95\": [0.021, 0.179]\n     }\n   }\n   ```\n\n7. **用 register_artifact 注册产出文件**\n\n### 关键提醒\n- 这是 CPU 分析任务，不要使用 GPU\n- 不要干扰正在运行的 C2 实验 (tmux session: c2_opt13b_a050_s42_fp32)\n- 如果找不到现成的分析脚本，可以参考 artifacts/permutation_granger.py 中的 PermG 实现自行组装\n- 分析完成后把关键数字写入 worker 输出（FPR 变化、CI 变化），方便我更新论文",

FPR: null-control rate (9 seeds, 54 pairs); TPR: detection rate at selected $\alpha$ levels (Pythia-410M, $T{=}10$, fp32). $K{=}3$ balances FPR control (11.1\%) against detection power (75\% at $\alpha{=}1.0$); $K{=}4$ eliminates false positives but reduces TPR to ${\leq}25\%$.}
/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0514_4/projects/distrib_canary_collapse/docs/paper/conclusion.tex:5:Method choice alone inflates null-control FPR from 7.4\% to 94.4\%, establishing that single-method analyses cannot reliably distinguish temporal precedence from artifacts.
/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0514_4/projects/distrib_canary_collapse/docs/paper/conclusion.tex:6:Our five-method comparison identifies PermG as the recommended detector, combining the lowest FPR (7.4\%, 9~seeds) with a steep onset from 0/8 at $\alpha{\leq}0.45$ to 6/8 at $\alpha{=}0.50$.
/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0514_4/projects/distrib_canary_collapse/docs/paper/discussion.tex:4:Our Granger-precedence claim is scoped to temporal predictive improvement~\citep{granger1969investigating,shojaie2022granger}: bivariate tests cannot exclude confounders, and the FPR CI ($[2.1\%, 17.9\%]$) means Type~I error may be non-negligible.
/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0514_4/projects/distrib_canary_collapse/docs/paper/method.tex:46:Pooled null-control FPR across nine seeds (54 pairs): PermG 7.4\%, DiffXCorr 14.8\%, Threshold 24.1\%, Toda--Yamamoto 33.3\%, NaiveXCorr 94.4\% (Appendix~\ref{app:fpr_independence}).
/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0514_4/projects/distrib_canary_collapse/docs/paper/method.tex:47:PermG (4/54; 95\% CI $[2.1\%, 17.9\%]$) achieves the lowest FPR, combining directional separation with consistent detection at $\alpha \geq 0.50$ (Shapley analysis: Appendix~\ref{app:shapley}).
/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0514_4/projects/distrib_canary_collapse/docs/paper/method.tex:70:FPR-inverse weighting penalizes high-FPR methods on null-control data: naive cross-correlation (FPR${=}94.4\%$) receives weight $w{=}1.06$, while PermG (FPR${=}7.4\%$) receives $w{=}13.5$.
/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0514_4/projects/distrib_canary_collapse/docs/paper/method.tex:76:Empirically, 4/54 null-control rejections across nine seeds confirm adequate size control (7/9 seeds yield 0~FP; Appendix~\ref{app:fpr_independence}).
/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0514_4/projects/distrib_canary_collapse/docs/paper/limitations.tex:11:PermG's empirical FPR is 7.4\% (CI $[2.1\%, 17.9\%]$) and $K{\geq}3$ consensus yields 11.1\% (6/54); per-condition detection rates carry wide binomial CIs (e.g., $[0.25, 0.92]$ for 6/8), so the phase diagram represents a coarse topology rather than precise probability estimates.
/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0514_4/projects/distrib_canary_collapse/docs/paper/appendix.tex:294:All thresholds trigger at generation~1, the same generation as entropy onset, because the gen-0$\to$gen-1 PPL jump is $+113\%$ (26.9$\to$57.4).
/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0514_4/projects/distrib_canary_collapse/docs/paper/appendix.tex:333:Our empirical FPR of 4/54 (7.4\%) confirms adequate size control.
/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0514_4/projects/distrib_canary_collapse/docs/paper/appendix.tex:392:PermG achieves the lowest FPR (4/54, 7.4\%; CI $[2.1\%, 17.9\%]$); all four FP occur in te$\to$distinct pairs in seeds~45/50 (7/9 seeds yield 0~FP).
/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0514_4/projects/distrib_canary_collapse/docs/paper/appendix.tex:399:\caption{Null-control FPR by method ($\alpha{=}0.0$, 9~seeds, 6~pairs each, BH-FDR $q{=}0.05$).}
/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0514_4/projects/distrib_canary_collapse/docs/paper/appendix.tex:406:\textbf{Perm.\ Granger} & \textbf{BH-FDR} & \textbf{4} & \textbf{7.4} \\
/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0514_4/projects/distrib_canary_collapse/docs/paper/appendix.tex:657:GPT-2-medium null-condition PermG FPR is 50\% (6/12 pooled, 2~seeds), dramatically higher than Pythia-410M (7.4\%), driven by ${\sim}4{\times}$ stronger null drift (entropy $-20.5\%$ vs.\ $-4.9\%$; distinct-3 $-35.8\%$ vs.\ $-4.9\%$).
/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0514_4/projects/distrib_canary_collapse/docs/paper/introduction.tex:22:Permutation Granger causality (PermG), the lowest-FPR method (7.4\%, CI $[2.1\%, 17.9\%]$, 9~seeds), reveals structured terrain rather than smooth degradation: detection is absent through $\alpha{=}0.45$, then jumps from 0/8 to 6/8 significant pairs within a 2-percentage-point interval (Figure~\ref{fig:phase_diagram}), before a non-monotonic dip and eventual reliable detection above an empirical boundary $\alpha_c(T)$ (Eq.~\ref{eq:alpha-c}).

95\\% CI $[2.1\\%, 17.9\\%]$) achieves the lowest FPR, combining directional separation with consistent detection at $\\alpha \\geq 0.50$ (Shapley analysis: Appendix~\\ref{app:shapley}).\n/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0514_4/projects/distrib_canary_collapse/docs/paper/method.tex:70:FPR-inverse weighting penalizes high-FPR methods on null-control data: naive cross-correlation (FPR${=}94.4\\%$) receives weight $w{=}1.06$, while PermG (FPR${=}7.4\\%$) receives $w{=}13.5$.\n/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0514_4/projects/distrib_canary_collapse/docs/paper/method.tex:76:Empirically, 4/54 null-control rejections across nine seeds confirm adequate size control (7/9 seeds yield 0~FP; Appendix~\\ref{app:fpr_independence}).\n/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0514_4/projects/distrib_canary_collapse/docs/paper/limitations.tex:11:PermG's empirical FPR is 7.4\\% (CI $[2.1\\%, 17.9\\%]$) and $K{\\geq}3$ consensus yields 11.1\\% (6/54); per-condition detection rates carry wide binomial CIs (e.g., $[0.25, 0.92]$ for 6/8), so the phase diagram represents a coarse topology rather than precise probability estimates.\n/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0514_4/projects/distrib_canary_collapse/docs/paper/appendix.tex:294:All thresholds trigger at generation~1, the same generation as entropy onset, because the gen-0$\\to$gen-1 PPL jump is $+113\\%$ (26.9$\\to$57.4).\n/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0514_4/projects/distrib_canary_collapse/docs/paper/appendix.tex:333:Our empirical FPR of 4/54 (7.4\\%) confirms adequate size control.\n/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0514_4/projects/distrib_canary_collapse/docs/paper/appendix.tex:392:PermG achieves the lowest FPR (4/54, 7.4\\%; CI $[2.1\\%, 17.9\\%]$); all four FP occur in te$\\to$distinct pairs in seeds~45/50 (7/9 seeds yield 0~FP).\n/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0514_4/projects/distrib_canary_collapse/docs/paper/appendix.tex:399:\\caption{Null-control FPR by method ($\\alpha{=}0.0$, 9~seeds, 6~pairs each, BH-FDR $q{=}0.05$).}\n/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0514_4/projects/distrib_canary_collapse/docs/paper/appendix.tex:406:\\textbf{Perm.\\ Granger} & \\textbf{BH-FDR} & \\textbf{4} & \\textbf{7.4} \\\\\n/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0514_4/projects/distrib_canary_collapse/docs/paper/appendix.tex:657:GPT-2-medium null-condition PermG FPR is 50\\% (6/12 pooled, 2~seeds), dramatically higher than Pythia-410M (7.4\\%), driven by ${\\sim}4{\\times}$ stronger null drift (entropy $-20.5\\%$ vs.\\ $-4.9\\%$; distinct-3 $-35.8\\%$ vs.\\ $-4.9\\%$).\n/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0514_4/projects/distrib_canary_collapse/docs/paper/introduction.tex:22:Permutation Granger causality (PermG), the lowest-FPR method (7.4\\%, CI $[2.1\\%, 17.9\\%]$, 9~seeds), reveals structured terrain rather than smooth degradation: detection is absent through $\\alpha{=}0.45$, then jumps from 0/8 to 6/8 significant pairs within a 2-percentage-point interval (Figure~\\ref{fig:phase_diagram}), before a non-monotonic dip and eventual reliable detection above an empirical boundary $\\alpha_c(T)$ (Eq.~\\ref{eq:alpha-c}).\n/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0514_4/projects/distrib_canary_collapse/docs/paper/introduction.tex:29:    Five methods yield null-control FPR from 7\\% to 94\\% ($\\kappa{=}0.185$); ```text
40	\end{figure}
41	
42	\subsection{Method Sensitivity Analysis}
43	\label{sec:mmct}
44	
45	We compare five lead-lag detection methods---naive cross-correlation (NaiveXCorr), differenced cross-correlation (DiffXCorr), threshold onset, permutation Granger causality (PermG), and Toda--Yamamoto~\citep{toda1995statistical}---in a multi-method comparison~\citep{simonsohn2020specification} (algorithm in Appendix~\ref{app:mmct}), calibrated against null-control ($\alpha{=}0.0$) data.
46	Pooled null-control FPR across nine seeds (54 pairs): PermG 7.4\%, DiffXCorr 14.8\%, Threshold 24.1\%, Toda--Yamamoto 33.3\%, NaiveXCorr 94.4\% (Appendix~\ref{app:fpr_independence}).
47	PermG (4/54; 95\% CI $[2.1\%, 17.9\%]$) achieves the lowest FPR, combining directional separation with consistent detection at $\alpha \geq 0.50$ (Shapley analysis: Appendix~\ref{app:shapley}).
48	Detection results use equal-weight majority voting ($K{\geq}3$ of 5 methods) with forward-direction filtering; $K{=}3$ yields 11.1\% FPR with 75\% true positive rate (TPR) at $\alpha{=}1.0$ (Table~\ref{tab:mmct_k_operating}).
49	The full MMCT procedure is given in Algorithm~\ref{alg:mmct}.
50	
51	\begin{algorithm}[t]
52	\caption{Multi-Method Consensus Test (MMCT)}
53	\label{alg:mmct}
54	\begin{algorithmic}[1]
55	\Require Time series pair $(X, Y)$; methods $\{m_1, \ldots, m_K\}$ ($K{=}5$); null-control data $\mathcal{N}$ (from $\alpha{=}0.0$); tolerance $\epsilon{=}0.01$
56	\Ensure $p$-value, consensus level $\hat{K}$
57	\For{each method $m_k$}
58	    \State Compute empirical FPR: $\widehat{\mathrm{FPR}}_k \gets$ fraction of null-control pairs where $m_k$ rejects $H_0$
59	    \State Compute weight: $w_k \gets 1 / \max(\widehat{\mathrm{FPR}}_k,\; \epsilon)$
60	    \State Apply $m_k$ to $(X, Y)$: $d_k \gets \mathbf{1}[m_k \text{ rejects } H_0]$
61	\EndFor
62	\State Weighted consensus: $W(X {\to} Y) \gets \sum_{k} w_k \, d_k \;/\; \sum_{k} w_k$
63	\State Build empirical null distribution of $W$ from all null-control pairs in $\mathcal{N}$
64	\State Compute $p$-value from empirical null: $p \gets \Pr_{\mathcal{N}}(W \geq W_{\mathrm{obs}})$
65	\State Consensus level: $\hat{K} \gets \sum_{k} d_k$
66	\State \Return $p$, $\hat{K}$
67	\end{algorithmic}
68	\end{algorithm}
69	
70	FPR-inverse weighting penalizes high-FPR methods on null-control data: naive cross-correlation (FPR${=}94.4\%$) receives weight $w{=}1.06$, while PermG (FPR${=}7.4\%$) receives $w{=}13.5$.
71	The empirical null distribution avoids parametric assumptions unreliable for short ($T{=}5$--$20$), non-stationary series.
72	
73	\paragraph{Permutation Granger causality.}
74	PermG tests whether past values of canary metric $X$ improve prediction of downstream metric $Y$ beyond $Y$'s own history~\citep{shojaie2022granger}, using 5{,}000 permutations~\citep{phipson2010permutation} on first-differenced series with Benjamini--Hochberg (BH) FDR correction at $q{=}0.05$ across 8 canary$\to$downstream pairs.
75	The permutation null controls Type~I error by construction; BH-FDR remains valid under positive regression dependence on a subset (PRDS)~\citep{benjamini2001control}.
76	Empirically, 4/54 null-control rejections across nine seeds confirm adequate size control (7/9 seeds yield 0~FP; Appendix~\ref{app:fpr_independence}).
77	
78	\FloatBarrier
79	
```

```text
1	\section*{Limitations}
2	\label{sec:limitations}
3	
4	\paragraph{Scope and generalizability.}
5	Our landscape characterization uses Pythia-410M as the primary architecture; the phase diagram is Pythia-family-only, frontier-scale behavior (${>}$7B) is untested, and cross-architecture evaluation is limited to $\alpha{=}1.0$.
6	All experiments use full replacement with a single decoding strategy (temperature~$1.0$, top-$k{=}50$); data accumulation~\citep{dey2024breaking} or lower temperatures would shift the landscape.
7	More fundamentally, the blind zone below $\alpha_c$ means that substantial quality degradation (e.g., ${\sim}40\%$ distinct-1 decline at $\alpha{=}0.25$) occurs before any statistical method signals detection.
8	The framework is therefore better characterized as a confirmation tool for ongoing collapse than an early-warning system.
9	
10	\paragraph{Statistical power.}
11	PermG's empirical FPR is 7.4\% (CI $[2.1\%, 17.9\%]$) and $K{\geq}3$ consensus yields 11.1\% (6/54); per-condition detection rates carry wide binomial CIs (e.g., $[0.25, 0.92]$ for 6/8), so the phase diagram represents a coarse topology rather than precise probability estimates.
12	
13	\paragraph{Causal interpretation.}
14	Permutation Granger testing establishes temporal precedence, not causation; our short-sequence regime ($T{\leq}25$) limits statistical power relative to econometric standards ($T{\geq}100$), and nonlinear alternatives to the linear PermG test yield no significant results in our setting (see \S\ref{sec:discussion}).
15	
```

<details><summary>↩ Tool result · `toolu_01YYuUc8HH1V1euKSsBs9UQF`</summary>

```text
1	\section{Conclusion}
2	\label{sec:conclusion}
3	
4	Lead-lag analysis in iterative training collapse is far more fragile than the current literature assumes.
5	Method choice alone inflates null-control FPR from 7.4\% to 94.4\%, establishing that single-method analyses cannot reliably distinguish temporal precedence from artifacts.
6	Our five-method comparison identifies PermG as the recommended detector, combining the lowest FPR (7.4\%, 9~seeds) with a steep onset from 0/8 at $\alpha{\leq}0.45$ to 6/8 at $\alpha{=}0.50$.
7	Token entropy exhibits robust one-generation-ahead precedence over diversity collapse (24/24 forward, 0/24 reverse at $\alpha{=}1.0$; ${\geq}6/8$ in 11/15 cross-architecture conditions).
8	The blind zone below $\alpha_c$ constrains practical deployment to settings with sufficient contamination; evaluation at ${>}$7B parameters remains open.
9	
```

18	\end{figure}
19	
20	We systematically map this landscape on Pythia-410M~\citep{biderman2023pythia} across 13 contamination levels with multi-seed replication (Figure~\ref{fig:phase_diagram}).
21	Our most consequential finding concerns method sensitivity: five statistical methods applied to identical data yield null-control FPR spanning 7\% to 94\% ($\kappa{=}0.185$; Figure~\ref{fig:method_comparison}), demonstrating that single-method analyses cannot reliably distinguish real signals from artifacts.
22	Permutation Granger causality (PermG), the lowest-FPR method (7.4\%, CI $[2.1\%, 17.9\%]$, 9~seeds), reveals structured terrain rather than smooth degradation: detection is absent through $\alpha{=}0.45$, then jumps from 0/8 to 6/8 significant pairs within a 2-percentage-point interval (Figure~\ref{fig:phase_diagram}), before a non-monotonic dip and eventual reliable detection above an empirical boundary $\alpha_c(T)$ (Eq.~\ref{eq:alpha-c}).
23	Cross-architecture evaluation at $\alpha{=}1.0$ across five families (345M--6.9B) supports forward detection in 11/15 conditions, with four non-confirming cases isolating distinct failure modes (\S\ref{sec:generalization}).
24	Our canary claim is scoped to diversity metrics (distinct-$n$~\citep{li2016diversity}); Granger precedence establishes temporal predictive improvement, not causation (\S\ref{sec:method}).
25	
26	Our contributions are:
27	\begin{enumerate}
28	    \item \textbf{We document method sensitivity as a primary finding} (\S\ref{sec:mmct}).
29	    Five methods yield null-control FPR from 7\% to 94\% ($\kappa{=}0.185$); PermG, the lowest-FPR method across all five (7.4\%, CI $[2.1\%, 17.9\%]$, 9~seeds), is selected as the primary detector.
30	
31	    \item \textbf{We map the detectability landscape} of iterative model collapse (\S\ref{sec:regime}--\ref{sec:dose_response}).
32	    Detection is absent through $\alpha{=}0.45$, then jumps from 0/8 to 6/8 within 2 percentage points; a detection boundary parametrized as $\alpha_c(T) \propto T^{-\gamma}$ ($\gamma \in [0.64, 2.08]$; Eq.~\ref{eq:alpha-c}) organizes three confirmed regimes (blind, onset, detection) plus one unexplained non-monotonic anomaly.
33	
34	    \item \textbf{We empirically validate a cascade ordering for canary metric selection} (\S\ref{sec:cascade}).
35	    Under mode amplification, entropy responds before diversity by ${\geq}1$ generation (Principles~\ref{thm:entropy_leads}--\ref{prop:div_lag}), confirmed in 14/15 experiments ($\alpha \geq 0.30$; GPT-2-medium s43 bidirectional).
36	\end{enumerate}

    "oldString": "Pooled null-control FPR across nine seeds (54 pairs): PermG 7.4\\%, DiffXCorr 14.8\\%, Threshold 24.1\\%, Toda--Yamamoto 33.3\\%, NaiveXCorr 94.4\\% (Appendix~\\ref{app:fpr_independence}).",
    "newString": "Pooled null-control FPR across ten seeds (60 pairs): PermG 6.7\\%, DiffXCorr 14.8\\%, Threshold 24.1\\%, Toda--Yamamoto 33.3\\%, NaiveXCorr 94.4\\% (Appendix~\\ref{app:fpr_independence}).",
    "originalFile": "\\FloatBarrier\n\\section{Method}\n\\label{sec:method}\n\n\\subsection{Cascade Ordering}\n\\label{sec:cascade}\n\nIf distributional collapse follows a predictable ordering across metrics, the earliest-responding metric can serve as a canary signal.\nWe formalize known metric properties---entropy as a continuous indicator of mode concentration, diversity as a threshold indicator of lexical coverage---under mode amplification~\\citep{shumailov2024curse,dohmatob2024tale} (derivations in Appendix~\\ref{app:cascade_theory}).\n\n\\begin{designprinciple}[Entropy responds before diversity]\\label{thm:entropy_leads}\nUnder mode amplification: \\textup{(a)} entropy can decrease while the $\\epsilon$-effective support $S_\\epsilon(p) {=} |\\{i: p_i > \\epsilon\\}|$ (hence distinct-$n$) is unchanged; \\textup{(b)} if distinct-$n$ decreases, entropy has already decreased.\n\\end{designprinciple}\n\nThe asymmetry arises because entropy responds to any mass redistribution (first-order), while diversity changes only when a token probability crosses the generation threshold~$\\epsilon$; the margin $\\gamma = \\min_{i: p_i > \\epsilon}(p_i - \\epsilon)$ quantifies how much shift diversity absorbs silently.\n\n\\begin{designprinciple}[Diversity lag $\\geq 1$ generation]\\label{prop:div_lag}\nIf the per-generation perturbation satisfies $\\alpha\\|\\mathcal{T}(p_t) - p_t\\|_\\infty < \\gamma(p_t, \\epsilon)$ through entropy onset $t_H$, then diversity onset $t_D \\geq t_H + 1$.\n\\end{designprinciple}\n\nECE co-moves with entropy at zero lag ($\\Delta\\mathrm{ECE} \\approx \\Delta C > 0$ whenever $\\Delta\\bar{H} < 0$; Appendix~\\ref{app:cascade_theory}).\nThe predicted cascade $\\{H, \\mathrm{ECE}\\} \\to \\mathrm{Distinct}$-$n$ holds in 14/15 experiments at $\\alpha \\geq 0.30$: entropy and ECE onset at generation~1, distinct-$n$ at generation~2 ($t_H{=}t_{\\mathrm{ECE}}{=}1$, $t_D{=}2$ uniformly; \\S\\ref{sec:regime}; Appendix~\\ref{app:moment_cascade}).\nThe assumption requires monotonic entropy decline; SmolLM3-3B (3/8 at $\\alpha{=}1.0$; Table~\\ref{tab:generalization}) provides an empirical counter-example where entropy decouples from diversity, violating the ordering.\nThe cascade is also specific to diversity metrics: first-differencing eliminates canary$\\to$perplexity associations entirely ($F{\\approx}0$, $p{=}0.99$; \\S\\ref{sec:regime}).\n\n\\subsection{Design Considerations}\n\\label{sec:pitfalls}\n\nWe use Granger precedence testing~\\citep{granger1969investigating} to assess whether past canary values improve prediction of downstream metrics---not mechanistic causation; bivariate Granger tests cannot exclude confounders~\\citep{shojaie2022granger}.\nLead-lag analysis in iterative model collapse is fragile along three axes:\n\\emph{Method sensitivity}: five detection methods applied to identical data yield forward significance rates spanning 0\\% to 92\\%, with $\\kappa{=}0.185$.\n\\emph{Spurious regression}: first-differencing eliminates canary$\\to$perplexity associations ($F{\\approx}0$, $p{=}0.99$) while preserving canary$\\to$diversity (24/24$\\to$24/24)~\\citep{granger1974spurious}.\n\\emph{Regime dependence}: the same Granger test shifts from 0/8 to 6/8 significant pairs within a 2-percentage-point interval.\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{figures/fig_method_comparison.pdf}\n\\caption{Method sensitivity: five methods yield dramatically different FDR-significant rates on identical data. PermG achieves 0\\% null-control FPR with 75\\% detection at $\\alpha{\\geq}0.50$; dashed line: 5\\% nominal.}\n\\label{fig:method_comparison}\n\\end{figure}\n\n\\subsection{Method Sensitivity Analysis}\n\\label{sec:mmct}\n\nWe compare five lead-lag detection methods---naive cross-correlation (NaiveXCorr), differenced cross-correlation (DiffXCorr), threshold onset, permutation Granger causality (PermG), and Toda--Yamamoto~\\citep{toda1995statistical}---in a multi-method comparison~\\citep{simonsohn2020specification} (algorithm in Appendix~\\ref{app:mmct}), calibrated against null-control ($\\alpha{=}0.0$) data.\nPooled null-control FPR across nine seeds (54 pairs): PermG 7.4\\%, DiffXCorr 14.8\\%, Threshold 24.1\\%, Toda--Yamamoto 33.3\\%, NaiveXCorr 94.4\\% (Appendix~\\ref{app:fpr_independence}).\nPermG (4/54; 95\\% CI $[2.1\\%, 17.9\\%]$) achieves the lowest FPR, combining directional separation with consistent detection at $\\alpha \\geq 0.50$ (Shapley analysis: Appendix~\\ref{app:shapley}).\nDetection results use equal-weight majority voting ($K{\\geq}3$ of 5 methods) with forward-direction filtering; $K{=}3$ yields 11.1\\% FPR with 75\\% true positive rate (TPR) at $\\alpha{=}1.0$ (Table~\\ref{tab:mmct_k_operating}).\nThe full MMCT procedure is given in Algorithm~\\ref{alg:mmct}.\n\n\\begin{algorithm}[t]\n\\caption{Multi-Method Consensus Test (MMCT)}\n\\label{alg:mmct}\n\\begin{algorithmic}[1]\n\\Require Time series pair $(X, Y)$; methods $\\{m_1, \\ldots, m_K\\}$ ($K{=}5$); null-control data $\\mathcal{N}$ (from $\\alpha{=}0.0$); tolerance $\\epsilon{=}0.01$\n\\Ensure $p$-value, consensus level $\\hat{K}$\n\\For{each method $m_k$}\n    \\State Compute empirical FPR: $\\widehat{\\mathrm{FPR}}_k \\gets$ fraction of null-control pairs where $m_k$ rejects $H_0$\n    \\State Compute weight: $w_k \\gets 1 / \\max(\\widehat{\\mathrm{FPR}}_k,\\; \\epsilon)$\n    \\State Apply $m_k$ to $(X, Y)$: $d_k \\gets \\mathbf{1}[m_k \\text{ rejects } H_0]$\n\\EndFor\n\\State Weighted consensus: $W(X {\\to} Y) \\gets \\sum_{k} w_k \\, d_k \\;/\\; \\sum_{k} w_k$\n\\State Build empirical null distribution of $W$ from all null-control pairs in $\\mathcal{N}$\n\\State Compute $p$-value from empirical null: $p \\gets \\Pr_{\\mathcal{N}}(W \\geq W_{\\mathrm{obs}})$\n\\State Consensus level: $\\hat{K} \\gets \\sum_{k} d_k$\n\\State \\Return $p$, $\\hat{K}$\n\\end{algorithmic}\n\\end{algorithm}\n\nFPR-inverse weighting penalizes high-FPR methods on null-control data: naive cross-correlation (FPR${=}94.4\\%$) receives weight $w{=}1.06$, while PermG (FPR${=}7.4\\%$) receives $w{=}13.5$.\nThe empirical null distribution avoids parametric assumptions unreliable for short ($T{=}5$--$20$), non-stationary series.\n\n\\paragraph{Permutation Granger causality.}\nPermG tests whether past values of canary metric $X$ improve prediction of downstream metric $Y$ beyond $Y$'s own history~\\citep{shojaie2022granger}, using 5{,}000 permutations~\\citep{phipson2010permutation} on first-differenced series with Benjamini--Hochberg (BH) FDR correction at $q{=}0.05$ across 8 canary$\\to$downstream pairs.\nThe permutation null controls Type~I error by construction; BH-FDR remains valid under positive regression dependence on a subset (PRDS)~\\citep{benjamini2001control}.\nEmpirically, 4/54 null-control rejections across nine seeds confirm adequate size control (7/9 seeds yield 0~FP; 95\\% CI $[2.1\\%, 17.9\\%]$) achieves the lowest FPR, combining directional separation with consistent detection at $\\alpha \\geq 0.50$ (Shapley analysis: Appendix~\\ref{app:shapley}).\nDetection results use equal-weight majority voting ($K{\\geq}3$ of 5 methods) with forward-direction filtering; $K{=}3$ yields 11.1\\% FPR with 75\\% true positive rate (TPR) at $\\alpha{=}1.0$ (Table~\\ref{tab:mmct_k_operating}).\nThe full MMCT procedure is given in Algorithm~\\ref{alg:mmct}.\n\n\\begin{algorithm}[t]\n\\caption{Multi-Method Consensus Test (MMCT)}\n\\label{alg:mmct}\n\\begin{algorithmic}[1]\n\\Require Time series pair $(X, Y)$; methods $\\{m_1, \\ldots, m_K\\}$ ($K{=}5$); null-control data $\\mathcal{N}$ (from $\\alpha{=}0.0$); tolerance $\\epsilon{=}0.01$\n\\Ensure $p$-value, consensus level $\\hat{K}$\n\\For{each method $m_k$}\n    \\State Compute empirical FPR: $\\widehat{\\mathrm{FPR}}_k \\gets$ fraction of null-control pairs where $m_k$ rejects $H_0$\n    \\State Compute weight: $w_k \\gets 1 / \\max(\\widehat{\\mathrm{FPR}}_k,\\; \\epsilon)$\n    \\State Apply $m_k$ to $(X, Y)$: $d_k \\gets \\mathbf{1}[m_k \\text{ rejects } H_0]$\n\\EndFor\n\\State Weighted consensus: $W(X {\\to} Y) \\gets \\sum_{k} w_k \\, d_k \\;/\\; \\sum_{k} w_k$\n\\State Build empirical null distribution of $W$ from all null-control pairs in $\\mathcal{N}$\n\\State Compute $p$-value from empirical null: $p \\gets \\Pr_{\\mathcal{N}}(W \\geq W_{\\mathrm{obs}})$\n\\State Consensus level: $\\hat{K} \\gets \\sum_{k} d_k$\n\\State \\Return $p$, $\\hat{K}$\n\\end{algorithmic}\n\\end{algorithm}\n\nFPR-inverse weighting penalizes high-FPR methods on null-control data: naive cross-correlation (FPR${=}94.4\\%$) receives weight $w{=}1.06$, while PermG (FPR${=}7.4\\%$) receives $w{=}13.5$.\nThe empirical null distribution avoids parametric assumptions unreliable for short ($T{=}5$--$20$), non-stationary series.\n\n\\paragraph{Permutation Granger causality.}\nPermG tests whether past values of canary metric $X$ improve prediction of downstream metric $Y$ beyond $Y$'s own history~\\citep{shojaie2022granger}, using 5{,}000 permutations~\\citep{phipson2010permutation} on first-differenced series with Benjamini--Hochberg (BH) FDR correction at $q{=}0.05$ across 8 canary$\\to$downstream pairs.\nThe permutation null controls Type~I error by construction; BH-FDR remains valid under positive regression dependence on a subset (PRDS)~\\citep{benjamini2001control}.\nEmpirically, 4/54 null-control rejections across nine seeds confirm adequate size control (7/9 seeds yield 0~FP; Appendix~\\ref{app:fpr_independence}).\n\n\\FloatBarrier\n\n\\subsection{Detection Boundary}\n\\label{sec:theory}\n\nWe summarize the observed boundary as an empirical parametrization:\n\\begin{equation}\n\\alpha_c(T) = A \\cdot T^{-\\gamma},\n\\label{eq:alpha-c}\n\\end{equation}\nUnder an empirical sub-linear signal model $S(\\alpha) = c \\cdot \\alpha^\\beta$ ($\\beta < 1$), the exponent satisfies $\\gamma = 1/(2\\beta)$:\n\\begin{equation}\n\\alpha_c(T) = A \\cdot T^{-1/(2\\beta)},\n\\label{eq:alpha-c-theory}\n\\end{equation}\nwhere $A = (z^* \\sigma / c)^{1/\\beta}$ and $z^* = \\Phi^{-1}(\\pi^*)$ (justification in Appendix~\\ref{app:alpha_c_proof}).\nThis parametrization summarizes the observed pattern with three free parameters fitted to 3--4 data points; the wide $\\gamma$ confidence interval ($[0.64, 2.08]$) precludes distinguishing sub-linear from super-linear decay.\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{figures/fig_detection_surface.pdf}\n\\caption{Detection rate surface over $\\alpha \\times T$ (PermG forward, 3~seeds). Dashed curve: fitted boundary $\\alpha_c(T)$. The blind zone ($\\alpha \\leq 0.45$) shrinks as $T$ increases.}\n\\label{fig:detection_surface}\n\\end{figure}\n\nCross-$T$ validation yields $\\gamma \\in [0.64, 2.08]$ (profile 95\\% CI); the qualitative conclusion---detection improves with longer observation---is robust to the specific $\\gamma$ value (cross-validation details and shape analysis in Appendix~\\ref{app:alpha_c_proof}).\nDense $\\alpha$ sampling favors a smooth sigmoid over a step function (Akaike information criterion difference $\\Delta\\mathrm{AIC}{=}-110$), but the fitted onset steepness ($k{=}15.7$) exceeds smooth-model predictions by $3$--$4{\\times}$ (Appendix~\\ref{app:power_sim}).\n",

    "newString": "PermG's empirical FPR is 6.7\\% (CI $[1.8\\%, 16.2\\%]$) and $K{\\geq}3$ consensus yields 10.0\\% (6/60)",
    "originalFile": "\\section*{Limitations}\n\\label{sec:limitations}\n\n\\paragraph{Scope and generalizability.}\nOur landscape characterization uses Pythia-410M as the primary architecture; the phase diagram is Pythia-family-only, frontier-scale behavior (${>}$7B) is untested, and cross-architecture evaluation is limited to $\\alpha{=}1.0$.\nAll experiments use full replacement with a single decoding strategy (temperature~$1.0$, top-$k{=}50$); data accumulation~\\citep{dey2024breaking} or lower temperatures would shift the landscape.\nMore fundamentally, the blind zone below $\\alpha_c$ means that substantial quality degradation (e.g., ${\\sim}40\\%$ distinct-1 decline at $\\alpha{=}0.25$) occurs before any statistical method signals detection.\nThe framework is therefore better characterized as a confirmation tool for ongoing collapse than an early-warning system.\n\n\\paragraph{Statistical power.}\nPermG's empirical FPR is 7.4\\% (CI $[2.1\\%, 17.9\\%]$) and $K{\\geq}3$ consensus yields 11.1\\% (6/54); per-condition detection rates carry wide binomial CIs (e.g., $[0.25, 0.92]$ for 6/8), so the phase diagram represents a coarse topology rather than precise probability estimates.\n\n\\paragraph{Causal interpretation.}\nPermutation Granger testing establishes temporal precedence, not causation; our short-sequence regime ($T{\\leq}25$) limits statistical power relative to econometric standards ($T{\\geq}100$), and nonlinear alternatives to the linear PermG test yield no significant results in our setting (see \\S\\ref{sec:discussion}).\n",

{

  "isSidechain": false,

    "newString": "the FPR CI ($[1.8\\%, 16.2\\%]$)",
    "originalFile": "\\section{Discussion}\\label{sec:discussion}\n\n\\paragraph{Scope and failure modes.}\nOur Granger-precedence claim is scoped to temporal predictive improvement~\\citep{granger1969investigating,shojaie2022granger}: bivariate tests cannot exclude confounders, and the FPR CI ($[2.1\\%, 17.9\\%]$) means Type~I error may be non-negligible.\nFour of fifteen cross-architecture conditions do not confirm forward detection (\\S\\ref{sec:generalization}), each isolating a distinct PermG assumption violation: precision sensitivity (GPT-2 bf16), seed dependence (Pythia-1.4B), and entropy decoupling (SmolLM3-3B).\nThese patterns require architecture-specific null calibration before deployment (Appendix~\\ref{app:extended_discussion}).\n\n\\paragraph{Granger precedence limitations.}\nOur observation sequences ($T{=}10$--$25$) are far shorter than econometric standards ($T{\\geq}100$); permutation testing avoids asymptotic assumptions but cannot recover power lost to short samples~\\citep{shojaie2022granger}.\nBivariate tests are susceptible to omitted variable bias; first-differencing removes shared linear trends but nonlinear confounds remain possible.\nPermG tests linear precedence only; transfer entropy (Appendix~\\ref{app:transfer_entropy}) yields 0/192 significant pairs.\nPartial correlation provides supporting evidence: after controlling for generation number, contemporaneous associations weaken ($\\bar{|r|}$: $0.590{\\to}0.381$) while lagged correlations strengthen ($0.759{\\to}0.841$), consistent with genuine temporal precedence rather than shared-trend artifacts (Appendix~\\ref{app:partial_corr}).\n\n\\paragraph{Method complementarity.}\nShapley value decomposition over all $2^5{=}32$ method coalitions (Appendix~\\ref{app:shapley}) reveals that DiffXCorr achieves the best combined Shapley contribution ($-0.299$) by detecting bidirectional coupling that directional methods miss, but cannot separate forward from reverse precedence.\nAmong directional methods, PermG ranks best ($-0.141$), combining low FPR with consistent detection.\nThe complementarity structure explains why consensus voting outperforms any single method: each method covers a distinct failure region of the others.\nPractitioners deploying MMCT must calibrate $K$ on architecture-specific null-control data, as the single viable operating point ($K{=}3$) reflects the specific method set and FPR profile of our five-method ensemble.\n\n\\paragraph{The $\\alpha{=}0.60$ anomaly.}\nThe non-monotonic detection gap at $\\alpha{=}0.60$ (\\S\\ref{sec:dose_response}) is consistent across seeds (0/8 for both tested seeds; Appendix~\\ref{app:alpha060}) and affects all directional methods while bidirectional DiffXCorr maintains detection, suggesting temporal lag compression defeats directional separation; recovery at $T{=}25$ (5/8) is consistent with $\\sqrt{T}$ power scaling.\n",
    "structuredPatch": [
      {
        "oldStart": 1,
        "oldLines": 7,
        "newStart": 1,
        "newLines": 7,
        "lines": [
          " \\section{Discussion}\\label{sec:discussion}",
          " ",

{
  "recorded_role": "user",
  "parentUuid": "04d4e4da-42c6-4a1a-a5fd-6a3a03c772a3",

    "originalFile": "\\section{Introduction}\n\\label{sec:introduction}\n\nWhen language models train iteratively on their own output, the training distribution progressively loses its tail modes, a phenomenon termed model collapse~\\citep{shumailov2024curse,alemohammad2024mad}.\nThis process can be triggered by vanishingly small synthetic fractions~\\citep{dohmatob2025strong}: lexical diversity narrows, calibration deteriorates, and each generation inherits the biases of its predecessors.\nAs machine-generated text grows prevalent on the web, such feedback loops pose a growing risk to production pipelines, and by the time standard quality metrics register degradation, the distribution may have already shifted irreversibly.\n\nThis threat motivates a fundamental question: \\emph{can we reliably detect the onset of model collapse?}\nThe answer is less clear than it might appear.\nIterative training produces short, noisy time series (often fewer than 15 points) where detection power drops sharply~\\citep{boettiger2012limits}, and model collapse is a first-order transition~\\citep{truong2026phase}, structurally ruling out the critical-slowing-down early-warning signals that ecologists rely on~\\citep{scheffer2009early} (\\S\\ref{sec:related_work}).\nMore fundamentally, no prior work has systematically compared detection methods on this problem; as we show, the choice of statistical test alone can shift conclusions from universal detection to complete null on identical data.\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{figures/fig_phase_diagram.pdf}\n\\caption{Phase diagram (Pythia-410M, fp32, PermG). FDR-significant canary$\\to$diversity pairs across $\\alpha$ and $T$; dashed curve: fitted boundary (Eq.~\\ref{eq:alpha-c}); $\\diamond$: non-monotonic anomaly; $\\star$: dissolution at $T{=}25$.}\n\\label{fig:phase_diagram}\n\\end{figure}\n\nWe systematically map this landscape on Pythia-410M~\\citep{biderman2023pythia} across 13 contamination levels with multi-seed replication (Figure~\\ref{fig:phase_diagram}).\nOur most consequential finding concerns method sensitivity: five statistical methods applied to identical data yield null-control FPR spanning 7\\% to 94\\% ($\\kappa{=}0.185$; Figure~\\ref{fig:method_comparison}), demonstrating that single-method analyses cannot reliably distinguish real signals from artifacts.\nPermutation Granger causality (PermG), the lowest-FPR method (7.4\\%, CI $[2.1\\%, 17.9\\%]$, 9~seeds), reveals structured terrain rather than smooth degradation: detection is absent through $\\alpha{=}0.45$, then jumps from 0/8 to 6/8 significant pairs within a 2-percentage-point interval (Figure~\\ref{fig:phase_diagram}), before a non-monotonic dip and eventual reliable detection above an empirical boundary $\\alpha_c(T)$ (Eq.~\\ref{eq:alpha-c}).\nCross-architecture evaluation at $\\alpha{=}1.0$ across five families (345M--6.9B) supports forward detection in 11/15 conditions, with four non-confirming cases isolating distinct failure modes (\\S\\ref{sec:generalization}).\nOur canary claim is scoped to diversity metrics (distinct-$n$~\\citep{li2016diversity}); Granger precedence establishes temporal predictive improvement, not causation (\\S\\ref{sec:method}).\n\nOur contributions are:\n\\begin{enumerate}\n    \\item \\textbf{We document method sensitivity as a primary finding} (\\S\\ref{sec:mmct}).\n    Five methods yield null-control FPR from 7\\% to 94\\% ($\\kappa{=}0.185$); PermG, the lowest-FPR method across all five (7.4\\%, CI $[2.1\\%, 17.9\\%]$, 9~seeds), is selected as the primary detector.\n\n    \\item \\textbf{We map the detectability landscape} of iterative model collapse (\\S\\ref{sec:regime}--\\ref{sec:dose_response}).\n    Detection is absent through $\\alpha{=}0.45$, then jumps from 0/8 to 6/8 within 2 percentage points; a detection boundary parametrized as $\\alpha_c(T) \\propto T^{-\\gamma}$ ($\\gamma \\in [0.64, 2.08]$; Eq.~\\ref{eq:alpha-c}) organizes three confirmed regimes (blind, onset, detection) plus one unexplained non-monotonic anomaly.\n\n    \\item \\textbf{We empirically validate a cascade ordering for canary metric selection} (\\S\\ref{sec:cascade}).\n    Under mode amplification, entropy responds before diversity by ${\\geq}1$ generation (Principles~\\ref{thm:entropy_leads}--\\ref{prop:div_lag}), confirmed in 14/15 experiments ($\\alpha \\geq 0.30$; GPT-2-medium s43 bidirectional).\n\\end{enumerate}\n",
    "structuredPatch": [
      {

          "-Permutation Granger causality (PermG), the lowest-FPR method (7.4\\%, CI $[2.1\\%, 17.9\\%]$, 9~seeds), reveals structured terrain rather than smooth degradation: detection is absent through $\\alpha{=}0.45$, then jumps from 0/8 to 6/8 significant pairs within a 2-percentage-point interval (Figure~\\ref{fig:phase_diagram}), before a non-monotonic dip and eventual reliable detection above an empirical boundary $\\alpha_c(T)$ (Eq.~\\ref{eq:alpha-c}).",

328	\label{app:nonstationarity}

330	Monte Carlo simulation (AR(1) DGP, $T{=}11$, 1{,}000 reps) shows PermG FPR inflates to ${\sim}10\%$ under strong non-stationarity ($\varphi{\geq}0.95$), while first-differencing maintains proper size ($4.2$--$6.6\%$) at the cost of reduced power (Table~\ref{tab:nonstationarity_sim}).
331	Our pipeline applies first-differencing before permutation testing, combining both advantages.
332	Under null conditions ($\alpha{=}0.0$), token entropy exhibits $\varphi{=}-0.51$ (far from the danger zone); first-differencing reduces distinct-1's high raw $\varphi$ ($0.81$--$0.97$, reflecting deterministic trend) to $-0.14$--$0.44$.
333	Our empirical FPR of 4/54 (7.4\%) confirms adequate size control.

[Director 指令] 两个追加任务（C2 不受影响，继续跑）：

### 1. s51 registry metrics 补写
Reviewer 指出 registry 条目缺 metrics 字段。补写：
```
metrics: {permg_fwd: "0/6", permg_rev: "0/6", robust_pairs_K3: 0, total_pairs: 6}
```
与其他 null seeds 格式对齐。

### 2. 论文 FPR 更新：9 seeds → 10 seeds
s51 加入后：
- PermG FPR: 4/54 = 7.4% → 4/60 = 6.67%
- K≥3 FPR: 6/54 = 11.1% → 6/60 = 10.0%
- Binomial CI 相应收窄

**全文搜索替换**（涉及 abstract.tex, introduction.tex, method.tex, experiments.tex, conclusion.tex, discussion.tex, appendix.tex）：
- "9 seeds" → "10 seeds"（或 "9~seeds" → "10~seeds"）
- "4/54" → "4/60"
- "7.4\%" → "6.7\%"（或 6.67%，保持与上下文精度一致）
- "6/54" → "6/60"
- "11.1\%" → "10.0\%"
- CI 重新计算（binomial exact CI for 4/60 和 6/60）
- "7/9 seeds yield 0 FP" → 检查 s51 是否也是 0 FP（是的，0/6），更新为 "8/10 seeds yield 0 FP"

注意：不要遗漏任何一处。建议先 grep 全部 "7.4" "4/54" "9.seed" "11.1" 确认所有出现位置。

编译验证后汇报。

{
  "recorded_role": "user",
  "parentUuid": "8cb82c88-af0d-4c36-8f50-8a5bc6f00ac1",
  "isSidechain": false,
  "promptId": "dc136539-ab92-4e0d-b98d-c5a4e77455ad",
  "type": "user",
  "uuid": "5d59abbc-abff-4e58-a403-a4cefe81847d",
  "toolUseResult": {
    "filePath": "/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0514_4/projects/distrib_canary_collapse/docs/paper/method.tex",
    "oldString": "$K{=}3$ yields 11.1\\% FPR",
    "newString": "$K{=}3$ yields 10.0\\% FPR",
    "originalFile": "\\FloatBarrier\n\\section{Method}\n\\label{sec:method}\n\n\\subsection{Cascade Ordering}\n\\label{sec:cascade}\n\nIf distributional collapse follows a predictable ordering across metrics, the earliest-responding metric can serve as a canary signal.\nWe formalize known metric properties---entropy as a continuous indicator of mode concentration, diversity as a threshold indicator of lexical coverage---under mode amplification~\\citep{shumailov2024curse,dohmatob2024tale} (derivations in Appendix~\\ref{app:cascade_theory}).\n\n\\begin{designprinciple}[Entropy responds before diversity]\\label{thm:entropy_leads}\nUnder mode amplification: \\textup{(a)} entropy can decrease while the $\\epsilon$-effective support $S_\\epsilon(p) {=} |\\{i: p_i > \\epsilon\\}|$ (hence distinct-$n$) is unchanged; \\textup{(b)} if distinct-$n$ decreases, entropy has already decreased.\n\\end{designprinciple}\n\nThe asymmetry arises because entropy responds to any mass redistribution (first-order), while diversity changes only when a token probability crosses the generation threshold~$\\epsilon$; the margin $\\gamma = \\min_{i: p_i > \\epsilon}(p_i - \\epsilon)$ quantifies how much shift diversity absorbs silently.\n\n\\begin{designprinciple}[Diversity lag $\\geq 1$ generation]\\label{prop:div_lag}\nIf the per-generation perturbation satisfies $\\alpha\\|\\mathcal{T}(p_t) - p_t\\|_\\infty < \\gamma(p_t, \\epsilon)$ through entropy onset $t_H$, then diversity onset $t_D \\geq t_H + 1$.\n\\end{designprinciple}\n\nECE co-moves with entropy at zero lag ($\\Delta\\mathrm{ECE} \\approx \\Delta C > 0$ whenever $\\Delta\\bar{H} < 0$; Appendix~\\ref{app:cascade_theory}).\nThe predicted cascade $\\{H, \\mathrm{ECE}\\} \\to \\mathrm{Distinct}$-$n$ holds in 14/15 experiments at $\\alpha \\geq 0.30$: entropy and ECE onset at generation~1, distinct-$n$ at generation~2 ($t_H{=}t_{\\mathrm{ECE}}{=}1$, $t_D{=}2$ uniformly; \\S\\ref{sec:regime}; Appendix~\\ref{app:moment_cascade}).\nThe assumption requires monotonic entropy decline; SmolLM3-3B (3/8 at $\\alpha{=}1.0$; Table~\\ref{tab:generalization}) provides an empirical counter-example where entropy decouples from diversity, violating the ordering.\nThe cascade is also specific to diversity metrics: first-differencing eliminates canary$\\to$perplexity associations entirely ($F{\\approx}0$, $p{=}0.99$; \\S\\ref{sec:regime}).\n\n\\subsection{Design Considerations}\n\\label{sec:pitfalls}\n\nWe use Granger precedence testing~\\citep{granger1969investigating} to assess whether past canary values improve prediction of downstream metrics---not mechanistic causation; bivariate Granger tests cannot exclude confounders~\\citep{shojaie2022granger}.\nLead-lag analysis in iterative model collapse is fragile along three axes:\n\\emph{Method sensitivity}: five detection methods applied to identical data yield forward significance rates spanning 0\\% to 92\\%, with $\\kappa{=}0.185$.\n\\emph{Spurious regression}: first-differencing eliminates canary$\\to$perplexity associations ($F{\\approx}0$, $p{=}0.99$) while preserving canary$\\to$diversity (24/24$\\to$24/24)~\\citep{granger1974spurious}.\n\\emph{Regime dependence}: the same Granger test shifts from 0/8 to 6/8 significant pairs within a 2-percentage-point interval.\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{figures/fig_method_comparison.pdf}\n\\caption{Method sensitivity: five methods yield dramatically different FDR-significant rates on identical data. PermG achieves 0\\% null-control FPR with 75\\% detection at $\\alpha{\\geq}0.50$; dashed line: 5\\% nominal.}\n\\label{fig:method_comparison}\n\\end{figure}\n\n\\subsection{Method Sensitivity Analysis}\n\\label{sec:mmct}\n\nWe compare five lead-lag detection methods---naive cross-correlation (NaiveXCorr), differenced cross-correlation (DiffXCorr), threshold onset, permutation Granger causality (PermG), and Toda--Yamamoto~\\citep{toda1995statistical}---in a multi-method comparison~\\citep{simonsohn2020specification} (algorithm in Appendix~\\ref{app:mmct}), calibrated against null-control ($\\alpha{=}0.0$) data.\nPooled null-control FPR across ten seeds (60 pairs): PermG 6.7\\%, DiffXCorr 14.8\\%, Threshold 24.1\\%, Toda--Yamamoto 33.3\\%, NaiveXCorr 94.4\\% (Appendix~\\ref{app:fpr_independence}).\nPermG (4/60; 95\\% CI $[1.8\\%, 16.2\\%]$) achieves the lowest FPR, combining directional separation with consistent detection at $\\alpha \\geq 0.50$ (Shapley analysis: Appendix~\\ref{app:shapley}).\nDetection results use equal-weight majority voting ($K{\\geq}3$ of 5 methods) with forward-direction filtering; $K{=}3$ yields 11.1\\% FPR with 75\\% true positive rate (TPR) at $\\alpha{=}1.0$ (Table~\\ref{tab:mmct_k_operating}).\nThe full MMCT procedure is given in Algorithm~\\ref{alg:mmct}.\n\n\\begin{algorithm}[t]\n\\caption{Multi-Method Consensus Test (MMCT)}\n\\label{alg:mmct}\n\\begin{algorithmic}[1]\n\\Require Time series pair $(X, Y)$; methods $\\{m_1, \\ldots, m_K\\}$ ($K{=}5$); null-control data $\\mathcal{N}$ (from $\\alpha{=}0.0$); tolerance $\\epsilon{=}0.01$\n\\Ensure $p$-value, consensus level $\\hat{K}$\n\\For{each method $m_k$}\n    \\State Compute empirical FPR: $\\widehat{\\mathrm{FPR}}_k \\gets$ fraction of null-control pairs where $m_k$ rejects $H_0$\n    \\State Compute weight: $w_k \\gets 1 / \\max(\\widehat{\\mathrm{FPR}}_k,\\; \\epsilon)$\n    \\State Apply $m_k$ to $(X, Y)$: $d_k \\gets \\mathbf{1}[m_k \\text{ rejects } H_0]$\n\\EndFor\n\\State Weighted consensus: $W(X {\\to} Y) \\gets \\sum_{k} w_k \\, d_k \\;/\\; \\sum_{k} w_k$\n\\State Build empirical null distribution of $W$ from all null-control pairs in $\\mathcal{N}$\n\\State Compute $p$-value from empirical null: $p \\gets \\Pr_{\\mathcal{N}}(W \\geq W_{\\mathrm{obs}})$\n\\State Consensus level: $\\hat{K} \\gets \\sum_{k} d_k$\n\\State \\Return $p$, $\\hat{K}$\n\\end{algorithmic}\n\\end{algorithm}\n\nFPR-inverse weighting penalizes high-FPR methods on null-control data: naive cross-correlation (FPR${=}94.4\\%$) receives weight $w{=}1.06$, while PermG (FPR${=}6.7\\%$) receives $w{=}14.9$.\nThe empirical null distribution avoids parametric assumptions unreliable for short ($T{=}5$--$20$), non-stationary series.\n\n\\paragraph{Permutation Granger causality.}\nPermG tests whether past values of canary metric $X$ improve prediction of downstream metric $Y$ beyond $Y$'s own history~\\citep{shojaie2022granger}, using 5{,}000 permutations~\\citep{phipson2010permutation} on first-differenced series with Benjamini--Hochberg (BH) FDR correction at $q{=}0.05$ across 8 canary$\\to$downstream pairs.\nThe permutation null controls Type~I error by construction; BH-FDR remains valid under positive regression dependence on a subset (PRDS)~\\citep{benjamini2001control}.\nEmpirically, 4/60 null-control rejections across ten seeds confirm adequate size control (8/10 seeds yield 0~FP; Appendix~\\ref{app:fpr_independence}).\n\n\\FloatBarrier\n\n\\subsection{Detection Boundary}\n\\label{sec:theory}\n\nWe summarize the observed boundary as an empirical parametrization:\n\\begin{equation}\n\\alpha_c(T) = A \\cdot T^{-\\gamma},\n\\label{eq:alpha-c}\n\\end{equation}\nUnder an empirical sub-linear signal model $S(\\alpha) = c \\cdot \\alpha^\\beta$ ($\\beta < 1$), the exponent satisfies $\\gamma = 1/(2\\beta)$:\n\\begin{equation}\n\\alpha_c(T) = A \\cdot T^{-1/(2\\beta)},\n\\label{eq:alpha-c-theory}\n\\end{equation}\nwhere $A = (z^* \\sigma / c)^{1/\\beta}$ and $z^* = \\Phi^{-1}(\\pi^*)$ (justification in Appendix~\\ref{app:alpha_c_proof}).\nThis parametrization summarizes the observed pattern with three free parameters fitted to 3--4 data points; the wide $\\gamma$ confidence interval ($[0.64, 2.08]$) precludes distinguishing sub-linear from super-linear decay.\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{figures/fig_detection_surface.pdf}\n\\caption{Detection rate surface over $\\alpha \\times T$ (PermG forward, 3~seeds). Dashed curve: fitted boundary $\\alpha_c(T)$. The blind zone ($\\alpha \\leq 0.45$) shrinks as $T$ increases.}\n\\label{fig:detection_surface}\n\\end{figure}\n\nCross-$T$ validation yields $\\gamma \\in [0.64, 2.08]$ (profile 95\\% CI); the qualitative conclusion---detection improves with longer observation---is robust to the specific $\\gamma$ value (cross-validation details and shape analysis in Appendix~\\ref{app:alpha_c_proof}).\nDense $\\alpha$ sampling favors a smooth sigmoid over a step function (Akaike information criterion difference $\\Delta\\mathrm{AIC}{=}-110$), but the fitted onset steepness ($k{=}15.7$) exceeds smooth-model predictions by $3$--$4{\\times}$ (Appendix~\\ref{app:power_sim}).\n",
